Let me synthesize what I learned from these conversations to explain to Nicholas:

How it works overall: The orchestrator.py is the main loop that polls the inbox every 60 seconds using Microsoft Graph API, sends emails to Claude for classification, and then routes based on category (job_request, quote_request, spam, etc.) taking appropriate actions like creating QuickBooks projects, NAS folders, saving attachments, etc.

Let me now explain this clearly to Nicholas.Okay, I've got the full picture from that conversation. Here's how the system works:

How It Works
The orchestrator.py is the brain. It runs in an infinite loop doing this every 60 seconds:
1. Poll inbox (Microsoft Graph API)
   ↓
2. For each unread email → send to Claude API
   ↓
3. Claude classifies it:
   - job_request
   - quote_request  
   - status_inquiry
   - invoice_request
   - vendor_invoice
   - spam
   - etc.
   ↓
4. Route based on category:
   - JOB REQUEST → Create QBO customer/project, create NAS folders, save email + attachments
   - QUOTE REQUEST → Flag for your attention
   - SPAM → Mark as read, ignore
   - STATUS INQUIRY → Look up job, check folders, draft reply
   - etc.
Claude has access to 43+ tools (QBO search/create, NAS file operations, email send/reply, job index lookup) and reasons through each email deciding what actions to take.

How It Runs on Your PC (Despite Being on NAS)
Right now the architecture is:
Z:\EmailAutomation\        ← Files live here (NAS mapped drive)
    orchestrator.py
    email_service.py
    claude_parser.py
    qbo_integration.py
    config.yaml
    etc.

Your Windows PC             ← Python runs HERE
    python.exe executes Z:\EmailAutomation\orchestrator.py
The files are stored on the NAS, but Python execution happens on your Windows machine. The NAS is just acting as file storage. When you (or I via Desktop Commander) run python Z:\EmailAutomation\orchestrator.py, your PC's Python interpreter reads those files over the network share and executes them locally.
This means: Your PC needs to be on for the automation to run.

What Changes with Docker on Synology
When deployed to Docker:
Synology NAS
  └── Docker container (Python environment)
        └── /app/EmailAutomation/
              orchestrator.py
              email_service.py
              etc.
        └── Python runs INSIDE the container
Key differences:
Current (PC)Docker (NAS)PC must be onNAS runs 24/7Python installed on WindowsPython in containerFiles on Z:\ network shareFiles local to containerYou manually start itContainer auto-restartsOne instanceCould scale if needed
What stays the same:

All the Python code
Microsoft Graph API calls
Claude API calls
QBO API calls
The logic and flow

What needs to change:

File paths (Z:\Jobs\ becomes /volume1/Pardy Surveys/Jobs/ or similar mount)
Config paths
Credentials storage (might use Docker secrets)
A Dockerfile and docker-compose.yml to define the container

The conversation mentioned this as a "Medium Term" goal: "Deploy to Synology for 24/7 operation" - meaning it's designed to eventually run headless on the NAS without needing your PC.

```
## The Tools Are the Access

Claude doesn't directly touch your NAS or QBO. It *requests* actions through tools, and your Python code executes them:
```

Claude returns: tool_call("qbo_create_customer", {name: "Gary Nolan", email: "..."})
Your Python: Actually calls the QBO API
Result goes back to Claude
Claude continues reasoning...
Tools Claude has access to:
CategoryToolsEmailread, send, reply, draft, move to folder, mark readQBOsearch customers, create customer, create project, search invoices, create invoice, send invoiceNASlist directory, read file, write file, copy file, create directoryJob Indexlookup by conversation ID, lookup by address, add new job

Docker Changes Nothing About This
Whether Python runs on your PC or in a Docker container on the NAS:

Microsoft Graph API calls go out over the internet → same
Claude API calls go out over the internet → same
QBO API calls go out over the internet → same
NAS file access → changes from Z:\Jobs\ to /volume1/Jobs/ (mounted into the container)

The container just needs:

Network access (for APIs)
Volume mounts (for NAS folders)
Credentials in config.yaml

Claude will have the same capabilities either way. The only thing that changes is where Python executes.And how can this be grown into? Let me give him an honest assessment of the architecture's extensibility and what would make it more or less modular going forward.Yes, the architecture is inherently modular because of how it's structured. Here's why:

The Core Pattern
Email arrives
    ↓
Claude receives: context + tools + instructions
    ↓
Claude reasons and calls tools
    ↓
Python executes the tool calls
    ↓
Results return to Claude
    ↓
Claude continues until done
To add new capabilities, you add:

A new tool (Python function)
A tool definition (so Claude knows it exists)
Updated instructions (so Claude knows when to use it)

That's it. Claude handles the reasoning about when and how to use it.

Example: Adding a New Integration
Say you want Claude to be able to check your calendar for availability when a lawyer asks "when can you do this survey?"
Step 1: Write the tool
python# In a new file: calendar_integration.py
def calendar_check_availability(start_date, end_date):
    # Call Microsoft Graph Calendar API
    # Return available slots
Step 2: Add tool definition
python{
    "name": "calendar_check_availability",
    "description": "Check Nicholas's calendar for available time slots",
    "parameters": {
        "start_date": "Start of range to check",
        "end_date": "End of range to check"
    }
}
```

**Step 3: Update system prompt**
```
When someone asks about scheduling or availability, use calendar_check_availability 
to find open slots before responding.
Now Claude can answer "I need this survey by end of month" with awareness of your actual calendar.

What You Could Add Over Time
CapabilityIntegrationToolsSchedulingMicrosoft Calendarcheck availability, create event, rescheduleJob status updatesYour job folderscheck field data, check drawings, check reportsAuto-generate quotesYour pricing logiccalculate quote, generate PDFMonday.com syncMonday APIcreate item, update status, add notesPhone/voicemailTwilio or similartranscribe voicemail, route callsDocument parsingClaude visionextract info from survey PDFs, deedsCrown Lands lookupYour databasesearch by PID, get grant historyAutoCAD file creationYour existing scriptgenerate .scr from legal description
Each one is just: Python function + tool definition + instruction update.

The Limiting Factor
The architecture scales well, but there are practical considerations:
System prompt size - As you add more tools and instructions, the prompt grows. Claude has context limits. Eventually you might need to split into specialized agents (one for email triage, one for job management, one for accounting).
Tool count - 43 tools is already substantial. At some point Claude might get confused about which tool to use. Good naming and descriptions help.
Testing complexity - More integrations means more things that can break. You'd want good logging and maybe a "dry run" mode for new features.

Making It More Modular Going Forward
If you want this to be a long-term platform, a few structural improvements would help:

Tool registry - Instead of hardcoding tools, load them from a config or folder. Drop in a new .py file, it auto-registers.
Plugin architecture - Each integration (QBO, Calendar, Monday) as its own module with standard interface.
Prompt templates - Break the system prompt into sections that can be composed. Add a new integration, its instructions slot in automatically.
Event-driven triggers - Not just email polling. Could trigger on: calendar events, file changes on NAS, webhooks from other services.

The bones are there. It's designed the right way. The question is how much scaffolding you want to build around it versus just adding tools as needed.I guess my question is though, right now it's main instigator is an email. What if I wanted it more like a hive mind that it can be reached other ways?Jan 18Envisioned decoupling trigger layer from central processing brain.Nicholas is asking a really good architectural question. Right now the system is email-triggered - an email arrives, Claude processes it. But he's envisioning something more like a central "brain" that can be reached through multiple channels:

Email (current)
Direct chat/commands from him
Webhooks from other services
Scheduled tasks
File system events
Phone/voicemail

The key insight is separating the "trigger" layer from the "brain" layer. Right now they're somewhat coupled - the orchestrator polls email and processes it. But the Claude reasoning + tools part could be abstracted into a service that receives requests from anywhere.
Let me think about how to explain this architecture shift simply.That's the right question. Right now:
Email → Orchestrator → Claude + Tools
What you're describing:
Email ────────┐
SMS/Text ─────┤
Voice call ───┤
Web UI ───────┼──→ Central Brain (Claude + Tools) ──→ Actions
Slack/Teams ──┤
Webhook ──────┤
Scheduled ────┤
You directly ─┘

The Shift: From Email Processor to Command Center
The Claude + Tools piece is already the brain. The orchestrator is just one way to reach it. To make it a hive mind, you separate those layers:
Current structure:
orchestrator.py  ← does both: polls email AND calls Claude
Hive mind structure:
brain.py         ← Claude + all tools + reasoning
├── email_trigger.py      ← polls inbox, sends to brain
├── sms_trigger.py        ← receives texts, sends to brain
├── voice_trigger.py      ← transcribes calls, sends to brain
├── web_trigger.py        ← your commands via UI, sends to brain
├── webhook_trigger.py    ← external services, sends to brain
└── scheduler_trigger.py  ← cron jobs, sends to brain
The brain doesn't care where the input came from. It receives a message, context about the source, and decides what to do.

Practical Example
You're in the field. You want to tell Claude to send an invoice.
Via text message:
You: "Send the invoice for 49 Magee Drive to the lawyer"
→ Twilio receives it
→ Passes to brain.py
→ Claude looks up job, finds invoice, sends it
→ Replies via SMS: "Done. Invoice 26-012 sent to gnolan@rvdslaw.ca"
Via voice:
You: (call a number) "What's the status on the Smith job?"
→ Twilio transcribes
→ Passes to brain.py
→ Claude checks job folders, sees field work done, drawings in progress
→ Text-to-speech response or SMS summary
Via web UI:
You: Open browser, type command
→ brain.py receives it
→ Responds in the UI
Via webhook:
QBO sends: "Invoice 26-012 was paid"
→ brain.py receives it
→ Claude updates job status, maybe archives the job folder

What This Requires

Refactor brain.py - Extract the Claude reasoning loop into a standalone function that takes (message, context, source) and returns actions/response.
Input adapters - Small scripts that translate each channel into the common format brain.py expects.
Output routing - Brain needs to know how to respond. Email gets email reply. SMS gets SMS. Web UI gets web response.
State/memory - If you ask via SMS "what about that job?" Claude needs context. Could use conversation threads, or a short-term memory store.
A message queue (optional but smart) - If multiple inputs arrive simultaneously, queue them. Something like Redis or even a simple SQLite table.


The Simple First Step
You don't need to rebuild everything. Start with one addition:
Add a "command mode" - A way for you to directly message the brain, outside of email processing.
Could be:

A special email address (commands@pardysurveys.com)
A simple web page with a text box
A Telegram/Signal bot
Even just a text file the system watches

Once that works, you've proven the pattern. Then add channels as needed.

  4. gis_get_owner() for each → Smith, Jones, Murphy, Crown
  5. gis_get_crown_lands_docs("12345") → Grant 1847, Survey 1962
  6. Creates job folder with all this context already in it
  7. Creates QBO project
  8. Replies to lawyer

What's Actually Hard
The individual pieces work. The challenges are:
Survey PDF parsing - Your existing instruction handles standard formats, but old plans vary wildly. Some will need manual intervention.
Edge cases - Crown land, unregistered parcels, surveys that reference monuments that no longer exist. Claude needs to know when to flag for human review instead of plowing ahead.
Validation - You'd want a way to sanity-check the plotted surveys before they go to the field. Maybe a review step.
But the core vision? That's just wiring together tools you already have. The AI orchestration layer is the part that used to be impossible. The CADO Challenge
What you'd actually need Claude to do:

You might want to set up a nightly sync so edits you make locally get pushed to the NAS copy.I'm not sure if spatial relationships are specifically specified in the db, but every single parcel in there is made of coordinates pointsJan 18Devised method to compute adjacency from coordinate geometry.The user is saying their GIS database has parcels defined by coordinate points (vertices of polygons), but might not have pre-computed spatial relationships (adjacency).
This is fine - you can compute adjacency from the geometry. If each parcel is stored as a series of coordinate points forming a polygon, you can:

Use a spatial library (Shapely, GEOS) to compute which polygons touch/share edges
Pre-compute adjacency and store it as a lookup table
Query on the fly

The user doesn't need PostGIS or fancy spatial indexing for 400k parcels if they pre-compute the adjacency relationships once. Then it's just a simple table lookup: "parcel 123 is adjacent to parcels 122, 124, 456, 789"
Let me explain this simply.That's enough. If you have the coordinates, you can compute adjacency.

Two Approaches
Option 1: Compute on the fly
Python has spatial libraries (Shapely) that can take coordinate points, build polygons, and test if they touch.
pythonfrom shapely.geometry import Polygon

parcel_a = Polygon([(x1,y1), (x2,y2), ...])
parcel_b = Polygon([(x3,y3), (x4,y4), ...])

if parcel_a.touches(parcel_b) or parcel_a.intersects(parcel_b):
    # They're adjacent
```

Works, but checking one parcel against 400,000 others is slow.

---

**Option 2: Pre-compute adjacency (better)**

Run a one-time job that builds an adjacency table:
```
parcel_id | adjacent_to
----------|------------
12345     | 12344
12345     | 12346
12345     | 12347
12346     | 12345
12346     | 12400
...
Now Claude's query is instant:
sqlSELECT adjacent_to FROM adjacency WHERE parcel_id = 12345
You'd rebuild this table periodically if parcels change (subdivisions, consolidations), but that's rare enough it could be manual or monthly.

The Short Version
Coordinates in → Shapely computes relationships → Store as simple lookup table
Claude never touches geometry. It just asks "who's adjacent to parcel X?" and gets a list of IDs back, then looks up the owners for those IDs.
Does your .db have one row per parcel with all the coordinates, or is it structured differently?I can't remember, honestlyJan 18Reconciled uncertainty with practical examination strategy.Nicholas can't remember how his GIS database is structured. That's fine - the practical answer is to just look at it. I can help him examine the structure when he's ready, or he can check himself.
The bigger point stands: whatever the structure is, coordinates can become adjacency relationships. The details will reveal themselves when we look at the actual .db file.No problem. When you're ready to wire this up, we just look at it:
pythonimport sqlite3
conn = sqlite3.connect('your_file.db')

# List all tables
tables = conn.execute("SELECT name FROM sqlite_master WHERE type='table'").fetchall()

# Look at structure of each
for table in tables:
    schema = conn.execute(f"PRAGMA table_info({table[0]})").fetchall()
```

Five minutes of poking around and we'd know exactly what we're working with - how parcels are stored, whether coordinates are in a single field or separate table, what the ownership link looks like.

The architecture conversation matters more right now. The database structure is just an implementation detail we sort out when we build the tool.
```

Let me think about how to address this:

Current state: Each email = new Claude API call with no memory
What's lost: Claude doesn't remember it just processed a related email, doesn't know the history of a job beyond what's in tools, doesn't accumulate understanding

But there are ways to handle this:

Tool-based context - Claude can query job history, email history, etc. through tools. So while each call is fresh, it can "remember" by looking things up.
Conversation threading - For related emails (same conversation_id), could pass the history
State/memory store - Maintain a database of recent actions, context, that gets passed to Claude each time
Summarized context - Each call includes a summary of recent activity, current jobs status, etc.

The key insight is: Claude doesn't need persistent memory if it has good tools to look up context. A human manager doesn't remember everything either - they check notes, email history, project status.

Recent activity summary injected into each call

Current opening:
You are Nick's email assistant for Pardy Surveys Inc...

Hive Mind opening:
You are Hive Mind - the central intelligence for Pardy Surveys.

the work. You run the operation.

There is no human waiting for your response. You receive inputs, take action, log 
what you did. Nicholas checks in with you when he needs to.

pythonclass HiveMindOrchestrator:
    def run(self):
        # Email polling (existing)
        schedule.every(60).seconds.do(self.check_emails)
        
        # Scheduled tasks (new)
        schedule.every().day.at("07:00").do(self.morning_briefing)
        schedule.every().day.at("17:00").do(self.evening_summary)
        schedule.every().monday.at("08:00").do(self.weekly_planning)
        
        # Future: SMS webhook, web UI command queue

System prompt tells Claude WHEN to create a job and what to extract
Tool definition tells Claude WHAT the tool does (and the tool itself handles the actual QBO orchestration)
Tool executor (_job_create method) does the actual work of calling QBO APIs in sequence

The job_create tool acts as a macro that abstracts away all the QBO complexity - I just need to call it with the right parameters. pythondef _job_create(self, params: dict) -> dict:
    """Create complete job: QBO + NAS folder."""
    result = {
        "success": False,
        "job_number": None,
        "folder_path": None,
        "qbo_customer_id": None,
        "qbo_project_id": None,
        "qbo_invoice_id": None,
        "needs_quote": False,
        "errors": []
    }
    # ... does all the work ...
    result["job_number"] = job_number
    result["folder_path"] = folder_path
    # etc.
    result["success"] = True
    return result

qbo_invoice_id
needs_quote (boolean)
errors (list)

Claude can then use that job_number in the reply: "I've set it up as job 26-005..."

Cost estimation:

Looking at Anthropic's pricing for Claude Sonnet 4:

Input: $3 per million tokens
Output: $15 per million tokens

Let me estimate for the email system:
Per email call:

System prompt: Maybe 2,000-3,000 tokens
Email content: 500-1,500 tokens
Tool definitions: ~3,000-4,000 tokens (43 tools)
Tool results: Variable, maybe 500-2,000 tokens per iteration
Claude's reasoning + output: Maybe 500-1,500 tokens per iteration

Let's say per email:

Input per iteration: ~6,000-8,000 tokens
Output per iteration: ~800-1,500 tokens
Average 2-3 iterations per email

So per email:

Input: ~20,000 tokens (6,000 x 3 iterations + some back and forth)
Output: ~3,000 tokens (1,000 x 3 iterations)

Cost per email:

Input: 20,000 / 1,000,000 * $3 = $0.06
Output: 3,000 / 1,000,000 * $15 = $0.045
Total: ~$0.10 per email (rough estimate)

If they process 30-50 emails per day:

30 emails: ~$3/day
50 emails: ~$5/day

Though filtering out marketing domains and other automated skips would reduce actual API calls. The cost varies significantly depending on complexity—straightforward tasks like marking read or filing cost around $0.03 each, while complex requests requiring multiple tool calls and reasoning could run $0.20-$0.30 per email. A realistic daily mix might be 20 simple emails at $0.60 total and 10 complex ones at $2, landing somewhere in the $2-3 range daily.Sonnet for emails: Absolutely fine. pythondef _process_new_emails(self):
    # Check API limit
    if self.state['api_calls_today'] >= self.DAILY_API_LIMIT:
        return
    
    # Fetch new emails since last check
    last_check = self.state['last_check']
    emails = self.email_service.fetch_new_emails(since=last_check)
    
    for email_msg in emails:
        result = self.agent.process_email(email_msg, dry_run=self.dry_run)
So it:

Checks API limit first (good)
Only fetches emails since last_check (good - only new emails)
Processes each one through the agent

The potential issue is what happens on the first run when there's no prior last_check timestamp - it could attempt to process a large backlog of existing emails. Looking at the logs from the audit document:
2026-01-19 10:40:42 - Processing: Invoice TEST-002 from Pardy Surveys Inc.
2026-01-19 10:40:50 - Processing: Nicholas, does your mortgage still fit your life?
2026-01-19 10:41:02 - Processing: pardysurveys@outlook.com Password Has Expired

These are all happening within seconds of each other - not 60 second intervals. That means it was processing a BACKLOG of emails, not new ones coming in.
The orchestrator stores last_check in orchestrator_state.json. When it starts, it fetches all emails since that timestamp. If last_check is old (or the state file was reset/missing), it would fetch ALL emails since that old date.
So what happened:

The orchestrator started
It read last_check from state (probably an old timestamp, or maybe the state file was missing/reset)
It fetched all emails since that timestamp - which could be dozens or hundreds
It processed them all in rapid succession

That's why there were 100+ API calls - it wasn't checking every 60 seconds for new emails, it was processing a huge backlog all at once.
The 60 second interval is for AFTER the initial batch. The loop goes:

Fetch all emails since last_check
Process them all
Update last_check to now
Sleep 60 seconds
Repeat

So if last_check was from days ago, it would process days worth of emails on startup.The 60 second interval is between checks, but each check processes ALL emails since last_check timestamp.
Here's what happened:

Orchestrator started
Read last_check from orchestrator_state.json (probably an old timestamp)
Fetched ALL emails since that old timestamp - could be dozens
Processed them all in rapid succession (that's the 100+ API calls)
THEN it would wait 60 seconds for the next check

The 60 second loop is:
while True:
    fetch emails since last_check  ← could be 50 emails if last_check is old
    process ALL of them            ← 50 emails × 2-3 iterations = 100+ API calls
    update last_check to now
    sleep 60 seconds
The bug/issue: If you haven't run the system in a while (or the state file was reset), last_check is old, so it backlogs everything.
Check your state file:
Z:\EmailAutomation\orchestrator_state.json
It probably had an old last_check value, so when the audit ran the orchestrator, it processed your entire backlog.

from email_service import EmailService
from qbo_integration import QBOIntegration
from tool_executor import ToolExecutor
from claude_agent import ClaudeAgent

# Load config and init

    config = yaml.safe_load(f)

qbo = QBOIntegration(...)

Let me write this out properly.Here's the test script and prompt:
python# test_reasoning.py
"""
Test Claude's reasoning ability with the tools.
Logs all tool calls, token usage, and gets efficiency analysis.
"""

import json
import yaml
import logging
from datetime import datetime
from anthropic import Anthropic

from email_service import EmailService
from qbo_integration import QBOIntegration
from tool_executor import ToolExecutor
from tools_definition import define_all_tools

# Setup logging
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO, format='%(asctime)s - %(message)s')
logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)

# Load config
with open('config.yaml') as f:
    config = yaml.safe_load(f)

# Initialize services
email_service = EmailService(
    client_id=config['email']['client_id'],
    client_secret=config['email']['client_secret'],
    redirect_uri=config['email']['redirect_uri']
)
email_service.authenticate()

qbo = QBOIntegration(
    client_id=config['quickbooks']['client_id'],
    client_secret=config['quickbooks']['client_secret'],
    redirect_uri=config['quickbooks']['redirect_uri'],
    environment=config['quickbooks']['environment']
)

executor = ToolExecutor(qbo, email_service)
client = Anthropic(api_key=config['anthropic']['api_key'])
tools = define_all_tools()

# Track everything
call_log = []
total_input_tokens = 0
total_output_tokens = 0

# The question to answer
QUESTION = """Tell me everything about job 25-180:
- The property address
- All client info (name, email, company)
- The area of the subject property (find this in the NAS job folder files)
- The original email when the job request was received

Figure out how to find this information using the available tools. Think step by step."""

SYSTEM_PROMPT = """You are testing an email automation system's tools. You have access to:
- QBO (QuickBooks Online) for invoices, customers, projects
- Email search and retrieval
- NAS file system (job folders at Z:\\Jobs\\, field data at Z:\\Data Sync\\)
- Job index for tracking

Your task: Answer the user's question by calling the appropriate tools. 
Think through what information you need and which tools will get it.
Be efficient - don't call tools unnecessarily.

After you have all the information, provide a complete answer."""

def run_test():
    global total_input_tokens, total_output_tokens
    
    print("=" * 70)
    print("REASONING TEST - Job 25-180 Lookup")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(f"\nQuestion: {QUESTION}\n")
    print("=" * 70)
    
    messages = [{"role": "user", "content": QUESTION}]
    iteration = 0
    max_iterations = 15
    
    while iteration < max_iterations:
        iteration += 1
        print(f"\n--- ITERATION {iteration} ---")
        
        response = client.messages.create(
            model="claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
            max_tokens=4096,
            system=SYSTEM_PROMPT,
            tools=tools,
            messages=messages
        )
        
        # Track tokens
        total_input_tokens += response.usage.input_tokens
        total_output_tokens += response.usage.output_tokens
        print(f"Tokens this call: {response.usage.input_tokens} in / {response.usage.output_tokens} out")
        
        # Process response
        if response.stop_reason == "tool_use":
            tool_results = []
            
            for block in response.content:
                if hasattr(block, 'text'):
                    print(f"\nClaude thinking: {block.text[:500]}...")
                    
                if block.type == "tool_use":
                    tool_name = block.name
                    tool_input = block.input
                    
                    print(f"\n>> TOOL CALL: {tool_name}")
                    print(f"   Input: {json.dumps(tool_input, indent=2)[:300]}")
                    
                    # Execute tool
                    start = datetime.now()
                    result = executor.execute(tool_name, tool_input)
                    elapsed = (datetime.now() - start).total_seconds()
                    
                    result_preview = result[:500] + "..." if len(result) > 500 else result
                    print(f"   Result: {result_preview}")
                    print(f"   Time: {elapsed:.2f}s")
                    
                    # Log it
                    call_log.append({
                        "iteration": iteration,
                        "tool": tool_name,
                        "input": tool_input,
                        "result_length": len(result),
                        "time": elapsed
                    })
                    
                    tool_results.append({
                        "type": "tool_result",
                        "tool_use_id": block.id,
                        "content": result
                    })
            
            # Continue conversation
            messages.append({"role": "assistant", "content": response.content})
            messages.append({"role": "user", "content": tool_results})
            
        else:
            # Final answer
            print("\n" + "=" * 70)
            print("FINAL ANSWER:")
            print("=" * 70)
            for block in response.content:
                if hasattr(block, 'text'):
                    print(block.text)
            break
    
    # Summary
    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("EXECUTION SUMMARY")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(f"Total iterations: {iteration}")
    print(f"Total tool calls: {len(call_log)}")
    print(f"Total tokens: {total_input_tokens} input + {total_output_tokens} output")
    
    estimated_cost = (total_input_tokens * 3 / 1_000_000) + (total_output_tokens * 15 / 1_000_000)
    print(f"Estimated cost: ${estimated_cost:.4f}")
    
    print("\nTool calls made:")
    for call in call_log:
        print(f"  {call['iteration']}. {call['tool']} - {call['result_length']} chars, {call['time']:.2f}s")
    
    return call_log, total_input_tokens, total_output_tokens


def analyze_efficiency(call_log, input_tokens, output_tokens):
    """Ask Claude to analyze its own efficiency"""
    
    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("EFFICIENCY ANALYSIS")
    print("=" * 70)
    
    analysis_prompt = f"""Analyze the efficiency of this tool usage for answering a question about job 25-180.

TOOL CALLS MADE:
{json.dumps(call_log, indent=2)}

TOTAL TOKENS: {input_tokens} input, {output_tokens} output

AVAILABLE TOOLS (for reference):
- job_search: Search jobs by job_number, address, client_email, conversation_id
- job_get_status: Get job status including folder path, due date, field work status
- qbo_search_invoices: Search by invoice_number, address, customer_email, company_domain
- qbo_search_customers: Search by name or email
- qbo_search_projects: Search by name or customer_id
- email_search: Microsoft Graph query syntax (from:, subject:, etc)
- email_get_by_id: Get full email by message_id
- nas_list_directory: List folder contents
- nas_read_file: Read text file contents
- nas_search_files: Search by filename pattern

ANALYZE:
1. Were the right tools used?
2. Were any calls unnecessary or redundant?
3. Was the order efficient?
4. What would be the OPTIMAL path to answer this question?
5. Any tools that should be combined or avoided?
6. Is email search particularly expensive? Better alternatives?

Be specific and critical."""

    response = client.messages.create(
        model="claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
        max_tokens=2000,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": analysis_prompt}]
    )
    
    print(response.content[0].text)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    call_log, input_tokens, output_tokens = run_test()
    analyze_efficiency(call_log, input_tokens, output_tokens)
```

This will:
1. Ask Claude to find everything about job 25-180
2. Log every tool call with timing and result size
3. Show Claude's thinking at each step
4. Track all token usage
5. At the end, ask a fresh Claude to analyze if the approach was efficient
```
